is empty rather than unresolved, so the binding constraint on every exploitability statement in this
paper is search power.

Two further instrument results matter for how later sections read.

\paragraph{Transcript inversion works, and it is not an attack.} Because the target team is
deterministic and published, an adversary can invert its transcript: for every ask the target made,
ask what deals would have produced that ask, and contract the posterior accordingly. Measured, this
contracts the deal posterior by $\sim$\vsevenBitsPerAsk{} bits per observed ask beyond the
certificates the rules already supply --- the first such measurement in this corpus, and one the
threat model's literature review found no external precedent for. Converting those bits into play is
worth \vsevenInversionUnfitted{}~points unfitted, the strongest single arm anyone built against the
deployed policy.

\textbf{It is not an exploit.} The excess tracks planted-edge size within
$\pm$\vsevenInversionTrack{} on all eight rungs of the calibration ladder, which is the signature of
a general strength gain rather than an exploitation instrument. And no linear handicap adds more
than \vsevenHandicapBits{}~bits per ask, so within this policy family there is \emph{no readability
handicap for a stochastic policy to buy}. A brief that asked for a stochastic policy conditional on
the inverter biting was therefore closed on its antecedent rather than skipped.

\paragraph{A cost figure the corpus had wrong by two orders of magnitude.} The record priced the
searching configuration at \vsevenCorpusCost$\times$ the blueprint. That figure describes the
\emph{unrestricted} search, which is not the point of the frontier anything was measured at. The
endgame-restricted operating point was re-measured at $\sim$\vsevenCostRecorded$\times$.
\S\ref{sec:results-cost} corrects that correction in turn.

\subsection{Open-ended adversary generation}
\label{sec:instrument-adversaries}

The second phase's constraint was that the searches must not share a bias --- \emph{fifteen runs of
one search are one run}. Every exploiter search in the corpus before it had been the same search: a
cross-entropy fit of a linear vector, maximising win rate, from one seed, against one target. The
phase built six adversary classes, three correlation regimes, and a taxonomy of channels, and
measured channel ceilings \emph{before} fitting anything against them.

\paragraph{The headline is negative and it is the reason this paper exists in the shape it does.}
Nothing built exploits the v0.6 frontier beyond the detection floor. The best measured exploitability
of the deployed policy remains an in-class figure of \vsevenBestExploit{}
$[\vsevenBestExploitLo, \vsevenBestExploitHi]$ over \vsevenBestExploitN{} games --- \emph{below} the
class floor of \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}. Seven independently fitted searches confirmed it; none cleared
its class floor.

Every arm that beat the deployed policy by more than the floor turned out, on measurement, to be a
\textbf{better policy rather than an exploit}: the target's own test-time search at
\vsevenSearchAsExploit{}, and a single out-of-class coordinate at \vsevenOneSwitchArm{} whose gain
survives against opponents sharing none of the target's machinery. The second of those is worth
dwelling on, because it is where this cycle's entire strength gain comes from: it is a
\emph{hand-set} coordinate found by an unfitted sweep, with no fit at all, and against the more
expensive frontier point its edge is \vsevenOneSwitchArmVsFmid{} --- negative. It is a strength gain
with a worst case, not an exploit.

\paragraph{A defect found in the target rather than an attack on it.} The declaration-urgency channel
the phase expected to headline has a measured ceiling of \vsevenUrgencyCeiling{}
$[\vsevenUrgencyCeilingLo, \vsevenUrgencyCeilingHi]$ --- dead as an attack. But the \emph{same}
channel is worth \vsevenUrgencyWorthLo{} to \vsevenUrgencyWorthHi{} points to a configuration that
simply switches it off. The adversary owns the smaller half of a two-point defect in the target.

\paragraph{A cliff nobody can reach, and a termination duty inherited by anyone who removes it.}
Behind the incumbent's event counter sits a \vsevenCliff{}-point cliff: past a threshold its
declaration rule drops its team-ownership floor and cashes half-suits at close to a coin flip. No
adversary reaches it --- the longest game any of them produced against an unmodified incumbent is
\vsevenCliffIncumbentTail{} events. But that escalation is also the policy's termination guarantee,
and a configuration that switches it off has a self-play tail of \vsevenCliffTail{} events. Removing
a defect here creates an obligation, and \S\ref{sec:configuration-keys} is how it was discharged.

\subsection{What these two phases established before any candidate was built}
\label{sec:instrument-established}

\begin{enumerate}
\item The v0.7 case rests on \textbf{beating the frontier, not on closing exploits}. There are no
      exploits above the floor to close.
\item The bar is \fcheap{}, fixed in advance.
\item The only thing found that beats the frontier is a configuration built from the frontier's own
      search plus one hand-set coordinate plus a switch --- a \emph{configuration change}, not an
      architecture. This is the object called \composite{} throughout this paper, and
      \S\ref{sec:against-composite} is the section in which it becomes decisive.
\item The instrument's floor is \vsevenPrimaryFloor{} points and will not improve by spending games.
\item The evaluation material is sealed, and the adversary population is split in half by a rule
      fixed in advance, with half of it encrypted (\S\ref{sec:methodology-seal}).
\end{enumerate}
%% --- end sections_v07/09-instrument ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/10-rejected ---
\section{The hypotheses that were explored and rejected}
\label{sec:rejected}

Five candidate architectures were built in isolated worktrees, gated before they were scored, and
certified against the side-channel definition by the harness of \S\ref{sec:methodology-side} rather
than by inspection. \textbf{One survived, and it is the configuration change of
\S\ref{sec:configuration} rather than an architecture.} The four that died are set out here with the
measurements that killed them, because each closes a standing entry in the project's design register
with a number instead of an argument, and because a closed direction is the part of this cycle that
does not depreciate.

Every figure in this section is on \emph{training} material, at the budget stated with it, and none
of it is certified: every pooled interval reported below has a lower bound under the
\vsevenPrimaryFloor{}-point floor of \S\ref{sec:methodology-floor}. They are measured edges, and the
distinction is kept.

\subsection{The declaration-allocation channel closes at exactly zero}
\label{sec:rejected-alloc}

The register's second-ranked entry, priority \vsevenLonePriority{}, held that the whole of \vsix{}'s
margin lives in the declaration channel and that roughly two points of it remained. Its named
mechanism was that the deployed allocator --- a product of marginals --- picks a different allocation
from the joint posterior's argmax often enough to matter.

\textbf{It does not.} Over $\sim$\vsevenLoneDecls{} recorded voluntary declarations, across two banks
and both urgency arms, the joint argmax and the marginal-product argmax select \emph{the same
allocation at every one}, with the joint score holding a strict opinion on \vsevenLoneStrictLo\%--\vsevenLoneStrictHi\% of genuinely ambiguous cases. The reason is algebraic rather than statistical: the sequential joint's first
chain-rule factor \emph{is} the marginal, and the re-normalisation that follows reweights the
survivors without reordering them.

Two further measurements close the direction rather than merely the mechanism. First, the
\emph{exact} joint maximiser is a worse decision rule than the deployed approximation on the
declaration decision itself: paired, it fixes \vsevenJointFixed{} declarations and breaks \vsevenJointBroken{}, a net
\vsevenJointWorseNet{}~pp, which converts to about \vsevenJointWorseWin{}~win-rate points. This is the
second independent instance in this corpus of the project's standing habit paying off --- ``exact
Bayesian inference'' is an assumption to test, not a property to assume --- the first being \vsix{}'s
finding that the exact posterior is a worse predictor of card location than the approximation the
policy deploys. Second, \vsevenJointFlatLo\%--\vsevenJointFlatHi\% of the error class sits in
flat-posterior states where the exact object is provably a coin flip, so most of what looked like
headroom is an information limit and not a policy defect.

\textbf{And the channel is largely downstream of a defect that the configuration change removes
anyway.} Deleting the urgency escalation removes \vsevenLoneUrgencyShare\% of the entire
allocation-error class and drops the oracle ceiling for a \emph{perfect} allocator from
\vsevenLoneCeilingBefore{} win-rate points to \vsevenLoneCeilingAfter{} --- below the detection floor.
The consequence is a composition constraint that this paper honours: a declaration-channel gain and
the urgency-off gain may never be added, because they are the same defect counted twice.

\subsection{A leaf evaluator fitted \vsevenKoneFold$\times$ better buys nothing}
\label{sec:rejected-leaf}

The inherited conditional held that the test-time search cannot be judged until its leaf evaluator is
rebuilt, because the shipped one is algebraically close to a rescaling of the hit probability. A leaf
was fitted for the purpose, and the statistic that matters --- \emph{between-candidate} $R^2$, the
only component a paired deviation rule can consume --- improved from \vsevenKoneRsqBefore{} to
\vsevenKoneRsqAfter{}, a \vsevenKoneFold$\times$ gain replicated out of sample on $\sim$\vsevenKoneLeaves{} leaves.

In play it is worth \vsevenKoneInPlay{} against the material control's \vsevenKoneControl{}, losing on
both banks individually. The diagnosis is conversion failure, not fit failure: the rule's deviation
rate is \vsevenKoneChangeLo{} for the material leaf and \vsevenKoneChangeHi{} for the retuned one, so
the deviation rule at the shipped shrinkage is \textbf{nearly leaf-invariant}. A better leaf is not
the lever.

The conditional itself is discharged separately, and by re-measurement rather than by the new
evaluator. The single underpowered cell the conditional rested on --- one \vsevenFullGameOldN{}-game cell at \vsevenFullGameOld{} --- re-runs at \vsevenTruncatedEndgame{} replicated in the endgame regime with
\vsix{}'s \emph{own} leaf. Full-game truncated search already works with the leaf the conclusion said
could not support it; it is simply at a dominated operating point.

\subsection{Fitting against a per-decision objective produces a worse policy}
\label{sec:rejected-perdecision}

The register's enabling entry held that a per-decision objective is necessary because the scoreboard
is too noisy to resolve small effects. \textbf{Its precision claim is confirmed and its fitting form
is killed}, and the two halves are independent.

The design effect of deal clustering was measured for the first time in this cycle, and a
one-point-equivalent effect resolves in \vsevenPerDecisionGamesLo{}--\vsevenPerDecisionGamesHi{} games
on the declaration channel against \vsevenScoreboardGames{} on the scoreboard: the precision claim
confirmed at \vsevenDesignEffectLo$\times$--\vsevenDesignEffectHi$\times$.

But all three self-oriented per-decision objectives, fitted at matched budget against a per-game
control, produced \emph{worse} policies: \vsevenKfourSelfDecl{}, \vsevenKfourSelfAsk{} and
\vsevenKfourSelfAlloc{}~points, every one replicated in sign and two of them clearing the floor as
certified negatives. Each fit moved its own proxy in the intended direction and lost anyway:
the declaration-oriented fit bought \vsevenKfourTradeGain{}~pp of declaration accuracy by paying
\vsevenKfourTradeCost{}~pp of ask accuracy, and the ask-oriented fit did the reverse. The two
per-decision channels trade against each other, so a fitter holding \vsevenCoordinates{} coordinates buys one out of the other. Keep the estimator; drop the objective.

\subsection{Nothing learned survives a free random tie-break}
\label{sec:rejected-tie}

\vsevenTieContested\% of contested ask decisions in \vsix{} tie bit-for-bit. The open question was
whether anything can be \emph{selected} inside that group, as opposed to merely randomised. A learned
re-ranker was built --- the corpus's first learned agent --- restricted to the tie group.

Against the deployed policy it scores \vsevenKfiveTieOnly{} pooled, which is indistinguishable from the free hash tie-break's \vsevenKfiveFreeTie{}; head to head against that free tie-break at \vsevenPhaseTwoCell{} games it is
\vsevenKfiveHeadToHead{} $\pm$ \vsevenKfivePm{}, replicated. \textbf{Nothing learned survives the free
random tie-break.} The correct control for anything operating inside the tie group is the
decorrelated policy and not the deployed one, which is a methodological point this paper inherits
and applies.

The same capture explains why. Setting every candidate's true advantage to exactly zero and feeding
the capture's real standard errors through the engine's real deviation rule reproduces the search's observed deviation rate to a \vsevenCurseRelErr\% relative error on a quantity the null was never fitted to: the
search's per-decision selection signal is $\sim$\vsevenCurseShare\% winner's curse on its own
Monte-Carlo draw, and $\sim$\vsevenCurseTieShare\% inside the tie group, where the rule applies no
shrinkage at all.

\subsection{Two directions closed on arithmetic before they were built}
\label{sec:rejected-arithmetic}

\paragraph{Forced-endgame allocation.} The register priced closing the entire forced-endgame accuracy
gap. Re-derived from this cycle's own capture it is worth \vsevenLthirteenClose{}~win-rate points ---
the register's own figure corrected \emph{upward} by a factor of two and still a fiftieth of the
detection floor. The one route by which it could have returned, an adversary driving incidence up, was measured and costs the adversary \vsevenLthirteenAdvCost{}~points.

\paragraph{A stochastic policy bought to reduce readability.} The brief made this conditional on the
white-box inverter biting. Transcript inversion contracts the deal posterior by $\sim$\vsevenBitsPerAsk{} bits per observed
ask --- the first such measurement in this corpus, and one the threat model's literature review
found no external precedent for --- and
converting those bits into play is worth \vsevenInversionUnfitted{}~points unfitted. But the excess
tracks planted-edge size within $\pm$\vsevenInversionTrack{} on all eight rungs, which is the
signature of a general strength gain and not of an exploitation instrument. And no linear handicap
adds more than \vsevenHandicapBits{}~bits per ask, so within this policy family \textbf{there is no
readability handicap for a stochastic policy to buy.} The antecedent fails and the limb is closed
rather than skipped.

\subsection{What the four kills leave}
\label{sec:rejected-leaves}

The register the cycle inherited described a policy with several points of accessible headroom in
four named places. Three of them are now closed with measurements and the fourth is confirmed as an
instrument rather than a lever. What remains as the located gain is a single coordinate found by an
unfitted sweep --- not by any of the architecture work --- and \S\ref{sec:results-attribution}
measures it on holdout. That is the barrier this paper names, and \S\ref{sec:conclusion} states it
as such.
%% --- end sections_v07/10-rejected ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/11-configuration ---
\section{What v0.7 changes}
\label{sec:configuration}

v0.7 is \textbf{a configuration of the \vsix{} policy family, not a new architecture}. That sentence
is the protocol's, written before the evaluation ran, and the evaluation did not disturb it. The
frozen object is \vsix{}'s fitted \vsevenVsixCoords{}-coordinate vector, one hand-set extended coordinate, three
switches that turn off machinery an earlier phase measured as defective, one new mechanism, and the
truncated test-time search that \vsix{} shipped switched off:

\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily\raggedright
\vsevenFrozenSpecWrapped{}
\end{quote}

\noindent
It is written to \filepath{engine/fishbot\_v07.json} by \filepath{engine/freeze\_config\_v07.py},
which \emph{executes} its round-trip assertion rather than printing it: the evaluation reconstructed
the spec from that file and never typed it. The artifact carries
\vsevenCoordinates{} resolved coordinates and the mirror pathology digest
\texttt{\vsevenMirrorDigest{}}.

\subsection{The five keys, and what each was expected to be worth}
\label{sec:configuration-keys}

\begin{description}

\item[\texttt{r12=25} --- half-suit contestation.] The information-denial coordinate
  \texttt{oppCertDonate}, found by an \emph{unfitted} coordinate sweep in phase~2 rather than by any
  architecture work. Phase~2 measured a single hand-set setting of it at \vsevenOneSwitchArm{}~points
  against the deployed policy over \vsevenPhaseTwoCell{} games with no fit at all. The protocol states in advance
  that this key carries the gain and that a holdout leave-one-out drop of roughly two points is the
  check: \vsevenTrainRtwelveLoo{} on training material. \S\ref{sec:results-attribution} reports what
  holdout gives.

\item[The endgame-truncated search.] \texttt{s1=1,det=12,cand=4,kappa=2.5,rbelief=indep,depth=12,maxq=26}
  --- machinery \vsix{} built, measured and shipped \emph{off}. It is a determinized search that
  reconstructs all six continuation players at their own information sets rather than making them
  clairvoyant, with a paired lower-confidence-bound deviation rule. Its leave-one-out value on
  training material is \vsevenTrainSearchLoo{}.

\item[\texttt{rtie=1} --- decorrelating the tie group.] \vsevenTieContested\% of contested ask
  decisions in \vsix{} tie bit-for-bit and are decided by an unstable \texttt{std::sort} order. This
  key replaces that order with a hash of the public event stream. Training leave-one-out
  \vsevenTrainRtieLoo{}.

\item[\texttt{pool=-1,oppfloor=-1,force=1000000,askfloor=-1} --- the urgency escalation off.] These
  switch off \vfive{}'s 220-event declaration clock. Behind that clock sits a
  \vsevenCliff-point cliff: past the threshold the declaration rule drops its team-ownership floor and
  cashes half-suits at close to a coin flip. No adversary ever reached it --- the longest game any
  phase-2 adversary produced against an unmodified incumbent is \vsevenCliffIncumbentTail{} events ---
  but a candidate that simply switches the clock off inherits its termination duty. Training
  leave-one-out \vsevenTrainUrgoffLoo{}.

\item[\texttt{stall=12} --- the replacement termination guarantee.] Escalate after twelve consecutive
  public events in which this seat's hard-certificate hash does not change. The threshold is established at zero game cost: over \vsevenStallMirrorGames{} mirror games \vsix{} never produces a no-progress run longer than
  \vsevenStallIncumbentRun{}, and a \emph{deadlocked} configuration --- the gate's negative control,
  not \frozen{} --- produces \vsevenStallFrozenRun{}. Any threshold in $[12, 60]$ is simultaneously
  unreachable in ordinary play and immediate in a deadlock; the separation between the two regimes is
  fifty-fold, though the threshold's own margin over observed ordinary play is twelve against six. Armed, its mirror digest is byte-identical to the same configuration
  without it: the rule's cost in ordinary play is not small, it is identically zero, because it never
  runs.

\end{description}

\noindent
The last three are individually indistinguishable from zero at the margin. \emph{As a group} they were
measured at \vsevenTrainGroup{} $[\vsevenTrainGroupLo, \vsevenTrainGroupHi]$ on training material,
replicated on both training banks, as a paired head-to-head against \composite{}. The protocol
forbids attributing that group's value to any one of them, and \S\ref{sec:results-attribution} keeps
that discipline. One further key, \texttt{m2=0}, was dropped from the freeze because its
leave-one-out drop is exactly zero; it is measured anyway, because the ablation lattice preregisters
the cell.

\subsection{What was deliberately not changed}
\label{sec:configuration-notchanged}

No fit of any kind was applied to the frozen configuration. Its vector is \vsix{}'s, found by
\vsix{}'s repaired optimiser under a minimax-regret objective over a four-member panel. The
architecture work of phase~3 produced five candidates and contributed one of them --- the defect
stack above --- and contributed it as a configuration change plus one small mechanism rather than as
an architecture. \S\ref{sec:rejected} is what happened to the other four, and it is the more valuable
half of the cycle.

The engine defect that the evaluation runs \emph{under} is also deliberate. Agents are constructed
once per thread and reused across every deal that thread is handed, and some per-agent state survives
\texttt{reset()} and is reachable only under the search. Phase~4 named the carrier ---
\texttt{\vsevenSsixCarrier{}}, the rollout engine, which \texttt{reset()} never touches --- and did
not fix it, because the one-line repair changes the play of every searching configuration by about one
deal in \vsevenSsixIncidence{}, and shipping that after the freeze is the one thing a freeze exists to
prevent. It is a named, quantified engine defect, every strength number in this paper is measured
under it, and \S\ref{sec:results-residual} measures it again on holdout material.
%% --- end sections_v07/11-configuration ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/12-protocol ---
\section{The protocol, and the seal}
\label{sec:protocol}

The evaluation was run from a cleared context against a protocol committed before any holdout bank had
been played and before the sealed adversary half had been decoded. The phase read that protocol and
nothing else from the v0.7 document set: not the candidate report, not the adversary report, not any
training log. Where a training measurement is quoted for comparison it is quoted \emph{from the
protocol itself}, which states its expectations in advance for exactly that purpose --- so agreement
is a check and disagreement is a finding.

\subsection{The material, and what had to be verified before anything was measured}
\label{sec:protocol-material}

agreement is between two binaries as well as two dates.}

The homogeneity gate of \S\ref{sec:methodology-side} is calibrated here: each planted channel fails

Three of the fourteen sealed adversaries are policies the rest of the panel already carries. They are
run anyway, because the protocol fixes the panel by name, and their agreement with the corresponding
scripted or frontier cell is used as a free determinism check
(\S\ref{sec:corrections-reproducibility}).

\subsection{The commit gate runs before any strength number}
\label{sec:protocol-gate}

A configuration that scores higher while failing the pathology rules is rejected, and the ordering is
not a formality: the corpus contains a configuration that scores
\vsevenScoreboardTrapWin\% against \vfour{} while carrying \vsevenScoreboardTrapDead\% dead asks and
losing \vsevenScoreboardTrapLimit\% of its games to the action limit. Win rate and soundness are
dissociable in this family.

\frozen{} passes all \vsevenGateRules{} rules: \vsevenGateDeadAsks\% provably-dead asks against a
0.10\% bar, longest dead run \vsevenGateLongestDead{}, mirror tail max \vsevenGateTailMax{} and p99
\vsevenGateTailPnn{}, and \vsevenGateSsix{} seat-isolation mismatches in \vsevenGateSsixNodes{}
audited decisions. Reported and not gated: \vsevenGateEvents{} events per game, \vsevenGateAskHit\%
ask hit rate, \vsevenGateMisdecl\% misdeclaration. The negative control fails
\vsevenNegFailCount{} rules --- \vsevenNegFails{} --- which are exactly the ones the protocol names in
advance, on \vsevenNegDeadAsks\% dead asks, a longest dead run of \vsevenNegLongestDead{} and a mirror
tail reaching \vsevenNegTailMax{}.

\subsection{What the protocol said would count as failure}
\label{sec:protocol-failure}

Seven conditions were written down in advance, ``because after the numbers are in every one of these
has a reading that makes it sound like something else''. They are reproduced in full in
Appendix~\ref{app:thresholds} and answered condition by condition in \S\ref{sec:against}. Two of them
came out against the release.

The protocol also fixed the primary bar. \fcheap{} is named rather than the deployed policy,
deliberately: the deployed policy ships its search off, beating it is the easier claim, and an
earlier phase had already shown that several unfitted one-switch deviations of the incumbent do it.
The v0.7 case had to be against the frontier.

And it fixed, in advance, the sentence to be used if the partner-regime ordering survived --- naming
the direction so it could not be chosen afterwards, and recording that on training material that
comparison goes \emph{against} the release. \S\ref{sec:results-partners} reports what happened and
uses the sentence.

\subsection{Deviations are recorded next to the result they affect}
\label{sec:protocol-deviations}

The protocol requires a deviation for anything the evaluation did that it does not specify: a cell
dropped, a cell added, a threshold read a particular way, a command whose flags differ. There are
\vsevenDeviations{} of them, \vsevenDeviationAdded{} adding cells or checks the protocol's own
questions turn out not to be computable without, and \vsevenDeviationCorrections{} of them
corrections. Appendix~\ref{app:deviations} is the full register, read out of the results
document rather than retyped. \vsevenCellsDropped{} preregistered cells were dropped and
\vsevenCellsAdded{} were added --- the added ones because two of the protocol's own questions are not
computable from the cells it names (Table~\ref{tab:battery}).
%% --- end sections_v07/12-protocol ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/13-results ---
\section{Results}
\label{sec:results}

Every measurement in this section comes from the artifacts listed in Appendix~\ref{app:repro} and is
emitted into this document by \filepath{engine/build\_tables\_v07.py --paper}. The one exception is
labelled where it occurs: the two \emph{training} rows of Table~\ref{tab:regimes} are quoted from the
protocol, which states them in advance, and are the only hand-entered numbers in the section. The battery is \vsevenScoredCells{} scored cells over \vsevenScoredGames{} games, with
\vsevenLimitCells{} cell carrying any action-limit hit.

\textbf{Scored cells carry no audit evidence, and this paper does not pretend otherwise.} The match
harness does not run the soundness audit, so \texttt{auditChecks} is \vsevenAuditChecks{} in all
\vsevenScoredCells{} of them: ``zero audit violations over a scored cell would be zero out of zero.
The engine-wide audit figure --- \vsevenVerifyViolations{} violations in \vsevenVerifyChecks{} checks
--- comes from the verification run of \S\ref{sec:engine-verify}, is quoted there and in
\S\ref{sec:protocol-material}, and is not restated as a property of this battery.

The order of this section is fixed by the project's reporting rule and not by what flatters the
release: \textbf{the worst case and the minimax regret come first, the aggregate comes last and is
labelled a diagnostic, and the partner-regime table appears whether or not it helps.}

\subsection{The panel: worst case and minimax regret}
\label{sec:results-panel}

\vsevenPanelCells{} cells. Four arms --- \frozen{}, \incumbent{}, \fcheap{} and \composite{} --- each
scored against the \emph{same} \vsevenPanelMembers{}-member panel on the \emph{same} two banks under
the same protocol: \vsevenPanelNear{} near-class members at \vsevenDealsNear{} deals a bank,
\vsevenPanelFar{} far-class archetypes at \vsevenDealsFar{}, and \vsevenPanelExpensive{} expensive
members at \vsevenDealsExpensive{}. All four arms carry a cell for all \vsevenPanelMembers{} members, which is what makes minimax
regret defined at all.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Worst cell per arm over the shared panel. This is the headline and it is printed first.}
\label{tab:worstcase}
{\small\begin{tabular}{llrrl}

Arm & Worst cell & Edge (pts) & 95\% CI & Per bank \\
\midrule
FROZEN & composite & $-0.04$ & $[-1.41, +1.33]$ & 7090001:+1.67\ \ 7090002:-1.75 \\
INCUMBENT & SEALED:X01xC3f & $-4.24$ & $[-4.86, -3.62]$ & 7090001:-3.86\ \ 7090002:-4.63 \\

\frozen{}'s worst cell is \vsevenWorstFrozen{}
$[\vsevenWorstFrozenLo, \vsevenWorstFrozenHi]$, against \incumbent{}'s \vsevenWorstIncumbent{}
$[\vsevenWorstIncumbentLo, \vsevenWorstIncumbentHi]$. The preregistered secondary claim that the worst
case is not catastrophic passes.

\textbf{That cell does not replicate, and this paper's own rule says to say so.} \frozen{}'s worst
cell is \vsevenWorstFrozenBankOne{} on \texttt{7090001} and \vsevenWorstFrozenBankTwo{} on
\texttt{7090002}, and it is attained at a different member on each
(\texttt{\vsevenWorstFrozenBankOneOpp{}} and \texttt{\vsevenWorstFrozenBankTwoOpp{}}). On one bank
\frozen{} has no negative cell anywhere on the panel. Under \S\ref{sec:methodology-intervals}'s sign
rule the pooled \vsevenWorstFrozen{} is \emph{not replicated}
(\vsevenWorstFrozenRepl{}), and the same criticism this paper makes of \incumbent{}'s
regret-attaining member below applies to its own headline cell. What survives replication is the
comparison the protocol actually gated on --- \frozen{}'s worst cell is far above \incumbent{}'s on
both banks --- not the value \vsevenWorstFrozen{} itself.

One caveat belongs beside that comparison rather than beneath it. \incumbent{}, \fcheap{} and
\composite{} each meet \emph{themselves} in the panel and so carry a free $0.00$ cell, which can only
lower a worst case; \frozen{} is not a panel member and carries none. Excluding self-cells,
\composite{}'s worst becomes
\vsevenWorstCompositeExSelf{} against \vsevenWorstCompositeExSelfOpp{} and the other three are
unchanged, so the S6 claim survives on that reading too. \textbf{But \composite{} has the better
worst cell on both readings} --- \vsevenWorstComposite{} with its free self-cell and
\vsevenWorstCompositeExSelf{} without --- so \frozen{} does not have the best worst case of the four
arms, only a far better one than the arm the protocol asked it to be compared against. Both readings
are printed because only printing the flattering one would be a choice.

\frozen{} is \textbf{\vsevenRegretFrozenRank{}rd of four on minimax regret}, at
\vsevenRegretFrozen{} against \vsevenRegretBest{}'s \vsevenRegretFcheap{} and \composite{}'s
\vsevenRegretComposite{}. The arm with the best worst case is not the arm with the best regret, and
this paper does not resolve that tension by choosing the metric that wins.

The regret is concentrated in the far archetypes, where the blueprint arms beat \frozen{} outright ---
on opponents every arm already beats by \vsevenFarSpanLo{} to \vsevenFarSpanHi{} points
(Table~\ref{tab:farpanel}). Against

$[\vsevenHardCompositeLo, \vsevenHardCompositeHi]$.

\incumbent{}'s attaining member is decided by \vsevenRegretIncumbentGap{} points over the
runner-up, against a half-width orders of magnitude larger, and it flips between banks: the regret \emph{value} is

\paragraph{The aggregate, printed last and as a diagnostic.}
Over the \vsevenPanelMembers{} shared cells --- \vsevenPanelDistinct{} distinct policies, three of
which the protocol names twice and which this weighted mean therefore counts twice --- the panel
means are \frozen{} \vsevenPanelMeanFrozen{},
\composite{} \vsevenPanelMeanComposite{}, \fcheap{} \vsevenPanelMeanFcheap{} and \incumbent{}
\vsevenPanelMeanIncumbent{}. The rule forbids leading with this and it has not been led with. It
points the opposite way from minimax regret, and both are in this paper because neither on its own
is what the panel measured. The full \vsevenPanelMembers{}$\times$4 table is
Appendix~\ref{app:panel}, so a reader can take the worst cell themselves rather than take this
paper's word for which it is.

The primary claim: \frozen{} against \fcheap{} is \textbf{\vsevenBtwoFcheap{}}~points
$[\vsevenBtwoFcheapLo, \vsevenBtwoFcheapHi]$ over \vsevenBtwoFcheapN{} games, sign replicating on both

$[\vsevenBtwoIncumbentLo, \vsevenBtwoIncumbentHi]$, \fmid{} by \vsevenBtwoFmid{}

\paragraph{Declaration accuracy is reported and carries no claim.}
The declaration-family floor of \S\ref{sec:methodology-floor} applies to the declaration-accuracy
column here. The harness emits declaration accuracy as a bare rate with \emph{no interval of any
kind}, so no interval can be quoted for it and no claim can be made about the declaration channel.
\frozen{} runs \vsevenDeclFrozenLo{}--\vsevenDeclFrozenHi{} across the five cells against opponents'
\vsevenDeclOppLo{}--\vsevenDeclOppHi{}. That is a diagnostic. Declining to claim is what discharges the
rule; clearing a bar is not.

\subsection{Attribution: is the gain located?}
\label{sec:results-attribution}

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{The attribution lattice. Add-one-in is measured from the deployed policy; leave-one-out is
the drop from \frozen{}. Reference cell \frozen{} against \incumbent{} on the lattice banks:
\vsevenBfiveWhole{} $[\vsevenBfiveWholeLo, \vsevenBfiveWholeHi]$.}
\label{tab:attribution}
{\small\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Component & Add-one-in from v0.6 & 95\% CI & Leave-one-out drop & 95\% CI \\
\midrule
r12=25 & $+2.18$ & $[+1.55, +2.81]$ & $+1.69$ & $[+0.80, +2.57]$ \\
the search & $+1.91$ & $[+1.38, +2.44]$ & $+0.57$ & $[-0.33, +1.46]$ \\
rtie=1 & $+0.71$ & $[+0.08, +1.34]$ & $+1.08$ & $[+0.20, +1.97]$ \\
urgency-off & $+1.37$ & $[+1.12, +1.63]$ & $+0.64$ & $[-0.25, +1.53]$ \\
stall=12 & $+0.00$ & $[+0.00, +0.00]$ & $+0.00$ & $[-0.89, +0.89]$ \\
\textit{m2=0} \textit{(not in the freeze)} & $+0.77$ & $[+0.66, +0.88]$ & --- & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

The protocol's first advance prediction --- that \texttt{r12=25} carries the gain, checkable as a
leave-one-out drop of ``roughly two points'' --- \textbf{holds on that check}: the holdout drop is
\vsevenLooRtwelve{} $[\vsevenLooRtwelveLo, \vsevenLooRtwelveHi]$, with the training figure
\vsevenTrainRtwelveLoo{} inside the interval, and an add-one-in of \vsevenAddInRtwelve{}. The
stronger sentence --- that this coordinate \emph{is} where the gain lives --- is not supported: the
drop's lower bound is \vsevenLooRtwelveLo{}, and \S\ref{sec:against-location} shows the
protocol's own one-third location test failing on the lattice's primary bank.

The second holds exactly: \texttt{stall=12} is worth \vsevenAddInStall{}
$[\vsevenAddInStallLo, \vsevenAddInStallHi]$ added and \vsevenLooStall{} removed --- bit-identical,
reproduced on holdout, in win rate and in events per game alike. The rung did not fire. The third is only half testable here: \texttt{m2=0} is worth \vsevenAddInMtwo{}
$[\vsevenAddInMtwoLo, \vsevenAddInMtwoHi]$ added to the blueprint on holdout --- an interval
excluding zero, and larger than \texttt{rtie=1}'s add-one-in --- while its \emph{zero leave-one-out
drop} was measured on training material and is not re-measured here, because the freeze does not
carry the key and the protocol says in terms not to add it back. There is no \texttt{L-m2} cell and
there could not be one.

The naive sum of the five components the freeze carries is \vsevenBfiveSum{}; the measured whole is
\vsevenBfiveWhole{}. The sub-additivity ratio is \vsevenSubAdditivity{}
$[\vsevenSubAdditivityLo, \vsevenSubAdditivityHi]$ --- propagating the reference cell's half-width
and the five add-one-in half-widths in quadrature --- and the phase-2 composition figure
\vsevenSubAddPhaseTwo{} lies inside it, so the two phases' composition is not distinguishable here. \textbf{Quoting the sum would overstate the configuration by
\vsevenSubAdditivityOverstate{} points.} The preregistered attribution claim passes: the components are
sub-additive and no single leave-one-out drop exceeds the whole.

\textbf{The protocol's own location test does not replicate}, and \S\ref{sec:against-location} treats
that as the failure the protocol says it is.

Beside it belongs the fact the ratio omits: v0.7's absolute changed-partner median is
\vsevenSoneMedian{} against \vsix{}'s \vsevenBaseMedian{}, and the ratio is smaller mainly because the
self-play row it divides by is more than six times larger. Both are true, and neither is the headline
alone.

A one-seat upgrade among \vsix{} partners is worth \vsevenOneSeatFive{} against a \vfive{} opponent
(predicted \vsevenTrainOneSeatFive{}) and \vsevenOneSeatSix{} against a \vsix{} opponent (predicted
\vsevenTrainOneSeatSix{}). That a single seat buys a large fraction of the three-seat gain is the
strongest evidence in this paper that the gain is individual rather than conventional.

Every row keeps its sign, every row replicates, and the largest excursion from the default row is

Two readings belong with the table. \texttt{--maxasks=360} is \emph{bit-identical} to the default
(\vsevenMaxasksIdentical{}): the 400-ask cap is never reached in this population, so the row measures
nothing and the legacy residual below is arithmetically legacy minus \emph{two} effective components
rather than three. And that residual --- legacy minus its isolable components, which the protocol
assigns to the forced-endgame willingness ladder because it has no flag of its own --- is
\vsevenLegacyResidual{} $[\vsevenLegacyResidualLo, \vsevenLegacyResidualHi]$, an interval
\vsevenLegacyResidualRatio$\times$ the size of the residual. The data are equally consistent with the
ladder contributing nothing.

0.08 & $+1.59$ $[+1.05, +2.13]$ & $-2.29$ $[-2.92, -1.67]$ & $-4.67$ $[-5.30, -4.05]$ & $\mathbf{+2.38}$ $[+1.50, +3.26]$ \\
0.11 & $+8.52$ $[+7.93, +9.12]$ & $+2.52$ $[+1.89, +3.16]$ & $-6.90$ $[-7.52, -6.28]$ & $\mathbf{+9.42}$ $[+8.54, +10.31]$ \\
0.15 & $+11.44$ $[+10.85, +12.03]$ & $+7.30$ $[+6.68, +7.93]$ & $-5.34$ $[-5.97, -4.71]$ & $\mathbf{+12.64}$ $[+11.75, +13.53]$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

Two of the three supra-floor rungs are recovered with a \emph{lower bound} above the
\vsevenPrimaryFloor{} floor. The third, the \texttt{0.08} rung, recovers \vsevenBnineHeightExcess{}
$[\vsevenBnineHeightExcessLo, \vsevenBnineHeightExcessHi]$ --- above the floor on the point estimate
but with an interval that straddles it, so on the lower-bound reading this paper applies to its own
primary claim it is \emph{not} recovered (\vsevenBnineHeightRecovered{}). Its planted cost,
\vsevenBnineHeightCost{} $[\vsevenBnineHeightCostLo, \vsevenBnineHeightCostHi]$, straddles the floor
too, so the rung is only supra-floor on a point estimate in the first place. The recovered size does
track the planted size monotonically across all four rungs, and the ladder is sharply non-linear,
with most of the damage arriving between the second and third. The sub-floor rung is \textbf{not} recovered ---
\vsevenBnineHfiveExcess{} $[\vsevenBnineHfiveExcessLo, \vsevenBnineHfiveExcessHi]$, below the floor with an
interval containing zero --- which is the control that has to fail and does.

Two qualifications belong beside it. An earlier phase measured this same excess at \vsevenSubFloorExcess{}
$[\vsevenSubFloorExcessLo, \vsevenSubFloorExcessHi]$ and this evaluation gets
\vsevenBnineHfiveExcess{}: the same conclusion from a point
estimate seven tenths of a point higher. And its upper bound \emph{grazes} the floor. The verdict is
unchanged --- the test is whether the edge is recovered, and it is not --- but the margin is thinner
than the earlier phase's, and a reader entitled to the caveat is entitled to it here rather than in a
footnote.

The identity control is exact: \frozen{} against itself is \vsevenIdentityRate{} with a zero-width
interval on both banks over \vsevenIdentityN{} games each (\vsevenIdentityExact{}). The fitted responders lose to \emph{unhandicapped} \frozen{} in all four rows, reproducing
\S\ref{sec:exploit}'s finding from a different fitting bank and against a handicapped target. The
objective is \emph{not} independent --- these responders and four of the eight adversary searches
both maximise win rate --- so this is a second sample, not a second method. And the three planted side channels each fail exactly the
tests the protocol names for them and no others (Table~\ref{tab:sidechannel}).

The first column reproduces the known engine defect on holdout material and reproduces its shape:

The denominators move between the two columns in \vsevenSsixDenomMoved{} of the cells. That is the

throughput figure at a fixed thread count, so the multiple is read off the panel without spending a
game on a separate benchmark.
